What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm here because I just feel like I'm always running hot, you know? Like I can't shut the motor off. It's always been a thing for me, but lately, it's been getting me into some trouble at the firehouse. I just can't seem to sit still or wind down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worried or anxious?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I don't sit around worrying about things. It's not an anxious feeling in my head, if that makes sense. It's more of a physical thing. I'm not stressed or scared, I just... I physically can't settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. Especially with the paperwork part of the job. I'll finish up an incident report and hand it in, thinking it's completely solid. But then the captain will call me back in and point out that I put down the wrong unit number, or I missed a time stamp, or I just completely skipped over some little box that actually matters for the safety review. It happens a lot, honestly. I just kind of rush through it without catching the small stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sitting still or feeling restless when you're not at the firehouse, like when you're at home or out with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even on my days off, I have a hard time doing quiet stuff with my family. Like, if we're supposed to be just hanging out or watching a movie... I just can't stay put. I'll start talking, or moving around, or I'll get up and go check on the tools in the garage. I almost always end up getting up before I’ve really been sitting for very long.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your restlessness or inability to sit still affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I mean, my wife might get a little annoyed when I can't just sit on the couch with her for a whole movie, but it hasn't caused any real arguments or anything like that. \n\nAnd with my friends... we're usually out doing something active anyway, or they're guys from the station who are just used to how I am. So, no, I haven't really noticed it causing any actual problems with my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt this way, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much as far back as I can remember. I don't really have big mood swings or anything. I might get a little frustrated if I'm stuck doing paperwork or waiting around for something to happen, but I don't get easily upset or have these intense shifts in my mood. I'm generally pretty even-keeled, just... always on the go.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I physically can't settle down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, I'm not entirely sure what kind of things you mean by that. If you're talking about making big decisions without thinking, no, I haven't really noticed myself doing that. \n\nI mean, at the firehouse we obviously have to react fast, but that's all based on our training. Outside of work... no, I wouldn't really say I do things on impulse.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to details or following through on instructions?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely have some trouble with details. Especially with the paperwork part of the job. I'll finish up an incident report and hand it in, thinking it's completely solid. But then the captain will call me back in and point out that I put down the wrong unit number, or I missed a time stamp, or I just completely skipped over some little box that actually matters for the safety review. It happens a lot, honestly. I just kind of rush through it without catching the small stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sitting still or feeling restless when you're not at the firehouse, like when you're at home or out with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even on my days off, I have a hard time doing quiet stuff with my family. Like, if we're supposed to be just hanging out or watching a movie... I just can't stay put. I'll start talking, or moving around, or I'll get up and go check on the tools in the garage. I almost always end up getting up before I’ve really been sitting for very long.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your restlessness or inability to sit still affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I mean, my wife might get a little annoyed when I can't just sit on the couch with her for a whole movie, but it hasn't caused any real arguments or anything like that. \n\nAnd with my friends... we're usually out doing something active anyway, or they're guys from the station who are just used to how I am. So, no, I haven't really noticed it causing any actual problems with my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt this way, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much as far back as I can remember. My mom actually brought it up recently—she says I was exactly like this back in grade school. I was always the kid getting out of my seat during class, or I'd just blurt out the answer before the teacher even finished asking the question. \n\nI was always losing my homework, too. She said she'd get notes sent home from the teachers all the time about my behavior and how scattered I was, even though they always said I was bright enough to actually do the work. So, yeah, it's definitely been going on since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have intense mood swings or get easily upset?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really have big mood swings or anything. I might get a little frustrated if I'm stuck doing paperwork or waiting around for something to happen, but I don't get easily upset or have these intense shifts in my mood. I'm generally pretty even-keeled, just... always on the go.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I definitely talk, especially if we're hanging around the firehouse, but I don't think I dominate the conversation or anything. I'm usually pretty good about listening to other people. I might interrupt sometimes if I get an idea and just need to get it out before I forget it, but I wouldn't say I talk excessively.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I struggle with that. I mean, my job at the firehouse is pretty structured, so I know what my duties are and I just get them done. Even outside of work, I don't really feel like I have a hard time figuring out how to set up a task or put things in order. I might jump into things a little fast, but actually organizing what needs to be done isn't really an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing any problems for you at work, besides the issues with paperwork?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it actually is. It’s... well, it's started costing me trust at the firehouse. I actually got pulled into a performance and safety meeting recently about it. It basically comes down to the crew feeling like they just can’t count on me to track the details consistently. And because of that, my shot at moving up into leadership feels like it’s slipping away right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been in a situation where your restlessness or inability to focus has led to a significant mistake or a failure to complete a critical task at the firehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does happen. Like, if someone gives me three or four station tasks to knock out, I’ll usually start really strong on the first one. But then... I don't know, I'll get pulled into something else, and I just completely lose track of the rest. Then hours later, I’ll find the hose inventory or the equipment checklist just sitting there on a clipboard, only half-done. So yeah, following through on all the instructions at once is definitely an issue.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your symptoms are affecting your overall well-being or quality of life?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that in a big-picture way. I mean, it's definitely stressful. Like I just mentioned, the situation at the firehouse is really getting to me. Knowing the guys feel like they can't rely on me with the details, and worrying about my career... that definitely takes a toll on my mood. But outside of work, I haven't really stopped to think about my overall quality of life. It's mostly just... frustrating to deal with, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I'll start talking, or moving around, or I'll get up and go check on the tools in the garage. I almost always end up getting up before I’ve really been sitting for very long.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your restlessness or inability to sit still affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I mean, my wife might get a little annoyed when I can't just sit on the couch with her for a whole movie, but it hasn't caused any real arguments or anything like that. \n\nAnd with my friends... we're usually out doing something active anyway, or they're guys from the station who are just used to how I am. So, no, I haven't really noticed it causing any actual problems with my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt this way, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much as far back as I can remember. My mom actually brought it up recently—she says I was exactly like this back in grade school. I was always the kid getting out of my seat during class, or I'd just blurt out the answer before the teacher even finished asking the question. \n\nI was always losing my homework, too. She said she'd get notes sent home from the teachers all the time about my behavior and how scattered I was, even though they always said I was bright enough to actually do the work. So, yeah, it's definitely been going on since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have intense mood swings or get easily upset?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really have big mood swings or anything. I might get a little frustrated if I'm stuck doing paperwork or waiting around for something to happen, but I don't get easily upset or have these intense shifts in my mood. I'm generally pretty even-keeled, just... always on the go.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I definitely talk, especially if we're hanging around the firehouse, but I don't think I dominate the conversation or anything. I'm usually pretty good about listening to other people. I might interrupt sometimes if I get an idea and just need to get it out before I forget it, but I wouldn't say I talk excessively.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I struggle with that. I mean, my job at the firehouse is pretty structured, so I know what my duties are and I just get them done. Even outside of work, I don't really feel like I have a hard time figuring out how to set up a task or put things in order. I might jump into things a little fast, but actually organizing what needs to be done isn't really an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing any problems for you at work, besides the issues with paperwork?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it actually is. It’s... well, it's started costing me trust at the firehouse. I actually got pulled into a performance and safety meeting recently about it. It basically comes down to the crew feeling like they just can’t count on me to track the details consistently. And because of that, my shot at moving up into leadership feels like it’s slipping away right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been in a situation where your restlessness or inability to focus has led to a significant mistake or a failure to complete a critical task at the firehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does happen. Like, if someone gives me three or four station tasks to knock out, I’ll usually start really strong on the first one. But then... I don't know, I'll get pulled into something else, and I just completely lose track of the rest. Then hours later, I’ll find the hose inventory or the equipment checklist just sitting there on a clipboard, only half-done. So yeah, following through on all the instructions at once is definitely an issue.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your symptoms are affecting your overall well-being or quality of life?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that in a big-picture way. I mean, it's definitely stressful. Like I just mentioned, the situation at the firehouse is really getting to me. But outside of work, I haven't really stopped to think about my overall quality of life. It's mostly just... frustrating to deal with, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily life activities outside of work that you feel you're not able to do or enjoy because of your restlessness or inability to focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. So, yeah, it definitely makes it tough to just enjoy that normal downtime with them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I almost always end up getting up before I’ve really been sitting for very long.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your restlessness or inability to sit still affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I mean, my wife might get a little annoyed when I can't just sit on the couch with her for a whole movie, but it hasn't caused any real arguments or anything like that. \n\nAnd with my friends... we're usually out doing something active anyway, or they're guys from the station who are just used to how I am. So, no, I haven't really noticed it causing any actual problems with my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt this way, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much as far back as I can remember. My mom actually brought it up recently—she says I was exactly like this back in grade school. I was always the kid getting out of my seat during class, or I'd just blurt out the answer before the teacher even finished asking the question. \n\nI was always losing my homework, too. She said she'd get notes sent home from the teachers all the time about my behavior and how scattered I was, even though they always said I was bright enough to actually do the work. So, yeah, it's definitely been going on since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have intense mood swings or get easily upset?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really have big mood swings or anything. I might get a little frustrated if I'm stuck doing paperwork or waiting around for something to happen, but I don't get easily upset or have these intense shifts in my mood. I'm generally pretty even-keeled, just... always on the go.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I definitely talk, especially if we're hanging around the firehouse, but I don't think I dominate the conversation or anything. I'm usually pretty good about listening to other people. I might interrupt sometimes if I get an idea and just need to get it out before I forget it, but I wouldn't say I talk excessively.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I struggle with that. I mean, my job at the firehouse is pretty structured, so I know what my duties are and I just get them done. Even outside of work, I don't really feel like I have a hard time figuring out how to set up a task or put things in order. I might jump into things a little fast, but actually organizing what needs to be done isn't really an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing any problems for you at work, besides the issues with paperwork?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it actually is. It’s... well, it's started costing me trust at the firehouse. I actually got pulled into a performance and safety meeting recently about it. It basically comes down to the crew feeling like they just can’t count on me to track the details consistently. And because of that, my shot at moving up into leadership feels like it’s slipping away right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been in a situation where your restlessness or inability to focus has led to a significant mistake or a failure to complete a critical task at the firehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does happen. I don't know, I'll get pulled into something else, and I just completely lose track of the rest. Then hours later, I’ll find the hose inventory or the equipment checklist just sitting there on a clipboard, only half-done. So yeah, following through on all the instructions at once is definitely an issue.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your symptoms are affecting your overall well-being or quality of life?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that in a big-picture way. Knowing the guys feel like they can't rely on me with the details, and worrying about my career... that definitely takes a toll on my mood. But outside of work, I haven't really stopped to think about my overall quality of life. It's mostly just... frustrating to deal with, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily life activities outside of work that you feel you're not able to do or enjoy because of your restlessness or inability to focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. But then the captain will call me back in and point out that I put down the wrong unit number, or I missed a time stamp, or I just completely skipped over some little box that actually matters for the safety review. It happens a lot, honestly. I just kind of rush through it without catching the small stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sitting still or feeling restless when you're not at the firehouse, like when you're at home or out with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even on my days off, I have a hard time doing quiet stuff with my family. Like, if we're supposed to be just hanging out or watching a movie... I just can't stay put. I'll start talking, or moving around, or I'll get up and go check on the tools in the garage. I almost always end up getting up before I’ve really been sitting for very long.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your restlessness or inability to sit still affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I mean, my wife might get a little annoyed when I can't just sit on the couch with her for a whole movie, but it hasn't caused any real arguments or anything like that. \n\nAnd with my friends... we're usually out doing something active anyway, or they're guys from the station who are just used to how I am. So, no, I haven't really noticed it causing any actual problems with my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt this way, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much as far back as I can remember. My mom actually brought it up recently—she says I was exactly like this back in grade school. I was always the kid getting out of my seat during class, or I'd just blurt out the answer before the teacher even finished asking the question. \n\nI was always losing my homework, too. She said she'd get notes sent home from the teachers all the time about my behavior and how scattered I was, even though they always said I was bright enough to actually do the work. So, yeah, it's definitely been going on since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have intense mood swings or get easily upset?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really have big mood swings or anything. I might get a little frustrated if I'm stuck doing paperwork or waiting around for something to happen, but I don't get easily upset or have these intense shifts in my mood. I'm generally pretty even-keeled, just... always on the go.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I definitely talk, especially if we're hanging around the firehouse, but I don't think I dominate the conversation or anything. I'm usually pretty good about listening to other people. I might interrupt sometimes if I get an idea and just need to get it out before I forget it, but I wouldn't say I talk excessively.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I struggle with that. I mean, my job at the firehouse is pretty structured, so I know what my duties are and I just get them done. Even outside of work, I don't really feel like I have a hard time figuring out how to set up a task or put things in order. I might jump into things a little fast, but actually organizing what needs to be done isn't really an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing any problems for you at work, besides the issues with paperwork?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it actually is. It’s... well, it's started costing me trust at the firehouse. I actually got pulled into a performance and safety meeting recently about it. It basically comes down to the crew feeling like they just can’t count on me to track the details consistently. And because of that, my shot at moving up into leadership feels like it’s slipping away right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been in a situation where your restlessness or inability to focus has led to a significant mistake or a failure to complete a critical task at the firehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does happen. Like, if someone gives me three or four station tasks to knock out, I’ll usually start really strong on the first one. But then... I don't know, I'll get pulled into something else, and I just completely lose track of the rest. Then hours later, I’ll find the hose inventory or the equipment checklist just sitting there on a clipboard, only half-done. So yeah, following through on all the instructions at once is definitely an issue.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your symptoms are affecting your overall well-being or quality of life?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that in a big-picture way. I mean, it's definitely stressful. Like I just mentioned, the situation at the firehouse is really getting to me. Knowing the guys feel like they can't rely on me with the details, and worrying about my career... that definitely takes a toll on my mood. But outside of work, I haven't really stopped to think about my overall quality of life. It's mostly just... frustrating to deal with, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily life activities outside of work that you feel you're not able to do or enjoy because of your restlessness or inability to focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. Even on my days off, I have a really hard time doing quiet stuff with my family. Like, if we try to just hang out and watch a movie or relax, I... I just can't stick with it. I start talking, or I'm moving around. Next thing I know, I'm getting up to go check on my tools in the garage or something before I've even been sitting for very long. So, yeah, it definitely makes it tough to just enjoy that normal downtime with them.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you didn't experience any of these symptoms?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my mom says I was like this back in grade school, too. She told me I was always out of my seat, blurting out answers in class, losing my homework... that kind of stuff. She said I was always getting notes sent home from the teachers, even though they all said I was bright enough to do the work. So, yeah, it's been pretty much my whole life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with details and following instructions is more of a problem for you than your restlessness and inability to sit still?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd say one is worse than the other, honestly. They both cause a lot of problems, just in different ways. The detail stuff and the instructions... that's what's really hurting me at the firehouse right now, making the guys not trust me with the equipment. But the restlessness is what messes up things at home, like I was saying about not being able to just sit and watch a movie with the family. So they both just kind of wreck different parts of my life. I couldn't really pick which one is a bigger issue.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I was always the kid getting out of my seat during class, or I'd just blurt out the answer before the teacher even finished asking the question. \n\nI was always losing my homework, too. She said she'd get notes sent home from the teachers all the time about my behavior and how scattered I was, even though they always said I was bright enough to actually do the work. I'm generally pretty even-keeled, just... always on the go.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I definitely talk, especially if we're hanging around the firehouse, but I don't think I dominate the conversation or anything. I'm usually pretty good about listening to other people. I might interrupt sometimes if I get an idea and just need to get it out before I forget it, but I wouldn't say I talk excessively.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I struggle with that. I mean, my job at the firehouse is pretty structured, so I know what my duties are and I just get them done. I might jump into things a little fast, but actually organizing what needs to be done isn't really an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing any problems for you at work, besides the issues with paperwork?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it actually is. It’s... well, it's started costing me trust at the firehouse. I actually got pulled into a performance and safety meeting recently about it. It basically comes down to the crew feeling like they just can’t count on me to track the details consistently. And because of that, my shot at moving up into leadership feels like it’s slipping away right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been in a situation where your restlessness or inability to focus has led to a significant mistake or a failure to complete a critical task at the firehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does happen. I don't know, I'll get pulled into something else, and I just completely lose track of the rest. But outside of work, I haven't really stopped to think about my overall quality of life. It's mostly just... frustrating to deal with, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily life activities outside of work that you feel you're not able to do or enjoy because of your restlessness or inability to focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. So, yeah, it definitely makes it tough to just enjoy that normal downtime with them.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you didn't experience any of these symptoms?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my mom says I was like this back in grade school, too. She told me I was always out of my seat, blurting out answers in class, losing my homework... that kind of stuff. She said I was always getting notes sent home from the teachers, even though they all said I was bright enough to do the work. Especially with the paperwork part of the job. I'll finish up an incident report and hand it in, thinking it's completely solid. But then the captain will call me back in and point out that I put down the wrong unit number, or I missed a time stamp, or I just completely skipped over some little box that actually matters for the safety review. It happens a lot, honestly. I just kind of rush through it without catching the small stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sitting still or feeling restless when you're not at the firehouse, like when you're at home or out with friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even on my days off, I have a hard time doing quiet stuff with my family. Like, if we're supposed to be just hanging out or watching a movie... I just can't stay put. I'll start talking, or moving around, or I'll get up and go check on the tools in the garage. I almost always end up getting up before I’ve really been sitting for very long.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your restlessness or inability to sit still affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I don't really think so. I mean, my wife might get a little annoyed when I can't just sit on the couch with her for a whole movie, but it hasn't caused any real arguments or anything like that. \n\nAnd with my friends... we're usually out doing something active anyway, or they're guys from the station who are just used to how I am. So, no, I haven't really noticed it causing any actual problems with my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt this way, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much as far back as I can remember. My mom actually brought it up recently—she says I was exactly like this back in grade school. I was always the kid getting out of my seat during class, or I'd just blurt out the answer before the teacher even finished asking the question. \n\nI was always losing my homework, too. She said she'd get notes sent home from the teachers all the time about my behavior and how scattered I was, even though they always said I was bright enough to actually do the work. So, yeah, it's definitely been going on since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have intense mood swings or get easily upset?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I don't really have big mood swings or anything. I might get a little frustrated if I'm stuck doing paperwork or waiting around for something to happen, but I don't get easily upset or have these intense shifts in my mood. I'm generally pretty even-keeled, just... always on the go.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I definitely talk, especially if we're hanging around the firehouse, but I don't think I dominate the conversation or anything. I'm usually pretty good about listening to other people. I might interrupt sometimes if I get an idea and just need to get it out before I forget it, but I wouldn't say I talk excessively.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I struggle with that. I mean, my job at the firehouse is pretty structured, so I know what my duties are and I just get them done. Even outside of work, I don't really feel like I have a hard time figuring out how to set up a task or put things in order. I might jump into things a little fast, but actually organizing what needs to be done isn't really an issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing any problems for you at work, besides the issues with paperwork?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it actually is. It’s... well, it's started costing me trust at the firehouse. I actually got pulled into a performance and safety meeting recently about it. It basically comes down to the crew feeling like they just can’t count on me to track the details consistently. And because of that, my shot at moving up into leadership feels like it’s slipping away right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been in a situation where your restlessness or inability to focus has led to a significant mistake or a failure to complete a critical task at the firehouse?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does happen. Like, if someone gives me three or four station tasks to knock out, I’ll usually start really strong on the first one. But then... I don't know, I'll get pulled into something else, and I just completely lose track of the rest. Then hours later, I’ll find the hose inventory or the equipment checklist just sitting there on a clipboard, only half-done. So yeah, following through on all the instructions at once is definitely an issue.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your symptoms are affecting your overall well-being or quality of life?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that in a big-picture way. I mean, it's definitely stressful. Like I just mentioned, the situation at the firehouse is really getting to me. Knowing the guys feel like they can't rely on me with the details, and worrying about my career... that definitely takes a toll on my mood. But outside of work, I haven't really stopped to think about my overall quality of life. It's mostly just... frustrating to deal with, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily life activities outside of work that you feel you're not able to do or enjoy because of your restlessness or inability to focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess so. Even on my days off, I have a really hard time doing quiet stuff with my family. Like, if we try to just hang out and watch a movie or relax, I... I just can't stick with it. I start talking, or I'm moving around. Next thing I know, I'm getting up to go check on my tools in the garage or something before I've even been sitting for very long. So, yeah, it definitely makes it tough to just enjoy that normal downtime with them.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you didn't experience any of these symptoms?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my mom says I was like this back in grade school, too. She told me I was always out of my seat, blurting out answers in class, losing my homework... that kind of stuff. She said I was always getting notes sent home from the teachers, even though they all said I was bright enough to do the work. So, yeah, it's been pretty much my whole life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with details and following instructions is more of a problem for you than your restlessness and inability to sit still?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd say one is worse than the other, honestly. They both cause a lot of problems, just in different ways. The detail stuff and the instructions... that's what's really hurting me at the firehouse right now, making the guys not trust me with the equipment. But the restlessness is what messes up things at home, like I was saying about not being able to just sit and watch a movie with the family. So they both just kind of wreck different parts of my life. I couldn't really pick which one is a bigger issue.\n\nDoctor: Are your family or friends ever embarrassed or frustrated by your behavior in social situations?\n\nPatient: I don't think they're really embarrassed by me... at least, they haven't said anything like that. My wife definitely gets frustrated, though, especially when I can't just relax with them or when I interrupt her while she's talking. But as far as going out with friends or being in social situations, I haven't really noticed anyone being embarrassed by how I act.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes in your work or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Especially at work. I mean, I’ll finish an incident report and think it’s completely solid, ready to go. But then... the captain will pull me aside and point out that I put down the wrong unit number, or I completely missed a time stamp. Or maybe I just skipped over some little box that actually matters a lot for the safety review. So, yeah, those kinds of careless mistakes happen a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with details and following instructions are primarily due to distractibility or more because you just don't like doing those kinds of tasks?\n\nPatient: I'd say it's definitely the distractibility. It's not that I just don't want to do those tasks or that I'm trying to avoid them. It's just... I get pulled away so easily. Like, a radio chirp, somebody walking past the bay, or even just a thought popping into my head about a call from yesterday—any of that can completely knock me off what I’m doing. And once I lose focus like that, it takes me forever to get back on track with whatever instructions or details I was trying to follow.\n\nDoctor: Is your difficulty with details and following instructions mostly because you get distracted easily?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was just saying, it's definitely the distraction. It's not that I'm trying to avoid doing the work or anything like that. It's just... I get pulled away by everything going on around me so easily, and then those little details just slip right past me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
